### R03 · reasoning · 25 words · clip 8.0 s · first detection −1.1 s from the end of the clip · coverage 92 %

> You've got a stream of a billion events and you need the top hundred most frequent keys. How would you do that in bounded memory?

**In-app answer 1** — live, +1.6 s → **acceptable** (correctness 2 · on_topic 2 · delivery 2)
_grader: Correct size-100 min-heap mechanics and names Space-Saving for strict bounded memory, though it leads with an unbounded count map._

heard: "You've got a stream of a billion events, you need the top 100 most frequent keys"

> I'd use a frequency hash map paired with a min-heap of size 100. As events stream in, I update the counts and maintain the heap so the 100th most frequent item stays at the root. If unique keys exceed available memory, I'd switch to a Space-Saving streaming algorithm to keep the memory footprint strictly bounded.

**Arm gemini-3.1-flash-lite_captured-low-r2** — TTFT 4.4 s, 70 words → **acceptable** (correctness 2 · on_topic 2 · delivery 2)
_grader: Count-Min Sketch plus a size-100 min-heap is a valid bounded-memory heavy-hitters approach, clearly explained._

> I would use the Count-Min Sketch algorithm combined with a Min-Heap to track the top 100 keys. ### R06 · verbal · 8 words · clip 3.4 s · first detection +1.3 s from the end of the clip · coverage 100 %

> How do you make a payment endpoint idempotent?

**In-app answer 1** — whisper, +1.6 s → **acceptable** (correctness 2 · on_topic 2 · delivery 2)
_grader: Standard idempotency-key pattern with atomic SETNX dedupe and cached-response replay; correct and concise._

heard: "How do you make a payment endpoint idempotent?"

heard: "How does target leakage sneak into a feature pipeline?"

> Target leakage usually sneaks in when features inadvertently include information that wouldn't be available at the exact moment of prediction. The most common culprit is temporal leakage—like including a status code or a timestamp that only gets populated after the target event happens. I also see it in aggregate features where we accidentally calculate statistics over the entire dataset instead of using a point-in-time snapshot. Finally, you have proxy variables, where a feature is essentially a direct consequence of the target, like including 'refund_processed' when you're trying to predict churn. I always enforce point-in-time joins in my pipelines to prevent this.

heard: "When is PRAUC better?"

> PR-AUC is the better choice whenever you’re dealing with significant class imbalance, particularly when the positive class is rare. ROC-AUC includes the true negative rate, which can be misleadingly high if you have a massive number of negatives, effectively masking poor performance on the minority class. In my churn modeling work, where churners were a small fraction of the base, PR-AUC was essential because it forced the model to focus on precision and recall for that specific, critical minority group.

**In-app answer 1** — whisper, +4.9 s → **acceptable** (correctness 2 · on_topic 2 · delivery 2)

heard: "A model says 70%. What does it take for that number to actually mean 70%?"

> For that 70% to be meaningful, the model needs to be well-calibrated. It means that if you group all the instances where the model predicted 70%, exactly 70% of those cases should actually turn out to be positive. Most models, especially complex ones like boosted trees or deep neural networks, tend to be overconfident, so you usually need to apply a calibration technique like Platt scaling or Isotonic regression on a held-out validation set to align those predicted probabilities with the actual observed frequencies.
